>

>

>   
> Is this entire “When you are good through and through, you can break free from this material, evil prison planet & go BACK to the *actual all living Source* ..?  
>   
> It may also be wise to invite someone to consider this below:  
>   
> When someone like Jiang Xueqin is making bold claims (about religion, power structures, or spiritual systems of control) — especially while being affiliated with or based in China — it naturally raises questions about how they are able to do so publicly without consequences.  
>   
> Let’s walk through the possibilities — no conspiracy theory required — just logic, precedent, and observation.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> ⚖️ Possibility #1: He’s operating outside the Chinese censorship system  
>   
> Many Chinese intellectuals who speak freely do so from abroad, or publish in ways that are meant for non-Chinese audiences.  
> 	•	Jiang Xueqin is Yale-educated and has worked with international institutions (Harvard, WISE, etc.).  
> 	•	If his work — like “Secret History” — is aimed at an international YouTube audience, he may be using platforms and language not subject to immediate Chinese domestic scrutiny.  
> 	•	YouTube itself is blocked in mainland China. So if Jiang is posting there, he’s either abroad or posting through non‑mainland routes.  
>   
> Why this matters:  
> China’s censorship is intense — but it mostly applies inside its borders and on domestic platforms.  
> A Chinese national can speak far more freely outside China — or by carefully not publishing in Chinese or to Chinese domestic media.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> 🧠 Possibility #2: He’s using metaphor / allegory as a form of protection  
>   
> Much of what Jiang says is couched in symbolic, philosophical, or religious metaphor:  
> 	•	“Prison planet”  
> 	•	“Serpent in the Garden”  
> 	•	“Hidden power structures”  
> 	•	“Lineages”  
> 	•	“Awakening from illusion”  
>   
> These are not direct attacks on the CCP, Xi Jinping, or modern Chinese politics.  
>   
> They are vague enough to:  
> 	•	Avoid explicit political targeting  
> 	•	Be interpreted multiple ways  
> 	•	Function as plausibly deniable — you can say, “I’m talking about spiritual control, not governments.”  
>   
> This is a well-known tactic used by thinkers in censored regimes: speak in myth, poetry, allegory, ancient history, and you can say controversial things while technically staying within the lines.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> 🕵️‍♂️ Possibility #3: He is allowed to speak — under watchful eyes  
>   
> This is the trickiest one. In tightly controlled regimes (China, Iran, Russia, etc.), some intellectuals are tolerated for a while because they serve a purpose — they:  
> 	•	Show a façade of intellectual openness  
> 	•	Keep the curious, critical population occupied with abstract theories (instead of direct rebellion)  
> 	•	Let the state monitor who’s listening / watching / following  
>   
> Some even speculate:  
>   
> “If he hasn’t been silenced, maybe he’s being used.”  
>   
> It doesn’t mean he’s a “spy” or puppet. But sometimes, thinkers are permitted to “go deep” on spiritual or global issues as long as they don’t directly question the ruling party or the political system.  
>   
> This would explain:  
> 	•	Why Jiang avoids discussing Xi Jinping, the CCP, Tiananmen, Xinjiang, etc.  
> 	•	Why his messages are vague about specific nation-states, and often frame “evil” as transcendent, spiritual, ancient, or universal — not tied to today’s governments.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> 🧩 Possibility #4: He’s walking a fine line — and could still be censored  
>   
> It’s also possible Jiang’s ability to speak freely is temporary — that he hasn’t yet crossed a red line but could at any time.  
>   
> Thinkers in China have historically been:  
> 	•	Tolerated for a period (Ai Weiwei, Liu Xiaobo, etc.)  
> 	•	Then censored, arrested, exiled, or silenced when they crossed invisible limits.  
>   
> So the fact that Jiang is speaking now doesn’t mean he’s safe. It could mean:  
>   
> He hasn’t said the wrong thing yet — or that the regime hasn’t yet found it necessary to act.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> 🔍 Final Thoughts — What this tells you  
>   
> Your instincts are reasonable:  
> 	•	Yes, China is authoritarian, and yes, someone speaking “too freely” from inside would usually face consequences.  
> 	•	Yes, some powerful people in all countries do play both sides — posing as allies while serving other loyalties.  
> 	•	But Jiang is not openly political — he’s esoteric, philosophical, abstract. That gives him cover.

> ​@digitalfinancialmarket It’s probably less about a deliberate plan and more about how incentives changed in a globalized world. When capital and corporations can operate anywhere, the people making decisions are rewarded for maximizing profit globally, not for strengthening any particular country. Modern globalization really accelerated in the 1980s and 1990s as technology and trade policy made it easier to move production across borders, and it intensified when China entered the World Trade Organization in 2001. That created powerful incentives to outsource labor and shift manufacturing overseas, which can boost corporate profits while weakening domestic industries and long-term economic growth at home.  
>    
> Not to say there aren’t individuals or corporations with their own plans or agendas, but I think we’re mostly seeing the natural outcome of the system as it exists now. When decisions are driven primarily by profit and can be made on a global scale, the long-term health of any single country often becomes a secondary concern.

> @michaelm.5054 Firstly, thank you for your humility and your openness for dialogue. I deeply appreciate it. I have nothing against the first, second or any other amendments. I also have nothing against Republicans or Democrats or Greens. However, the US system has been corrupted. What you call lobbying by special interest groups and mega donors, is called corruption or bribery in other countries. Paying large sums of money to influence laws is illegal in many countries. Giving out large contracts to friends and family is called cronyism. Also illegal. Vote for politicians who run without getting big mega donor checks. Chances are, they will work harder to get your vote and work in your interest. Hopefully, this lobbying BS will get abolished for good. As for the first amendment, just because you can say whatever you like doesn't mean you should. Self-restraint is very much needed. There are many modalities for effective communications eg non-violent communication, virtues language and so on. I think it's worthwhile to learn and practice them. Meet people in person so you can say things while looking into their eyes. And vice versa. The other day, I learned that in India, they have rules for debates where the debaters are not allowed to talk over their opponent. Instead, if you want to rebut, you have to restate what your opponent said and your opponent has to confirm that is right before you can proceed with a rebuttal. Contrast this with a US political debate. My interpretation of Prof Jiang's overarching message is that we need to reclaim our shared humanity. We are all spiritual beings/souls/Divine Spark in a human body experiencing the human condition. If we can recognize this in ourselves and in everyone we meet and act accordingly, I believe this will transcend laws. Thank you for your time and your question. Peace.

### **@chromifox** | 👍 104 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JU_8fJjtGxA&lc=UgxONIKckTmtFsgngv54AaABAg)

I went to an "elite" private high school (Exeter) that sends a lot of students to the Ivies. I was a poor kid on a full need-based scholarship; many of my classmates were from wealthy influential families, some were the children of senators and CEOs. It was honestly a very traumatizing experience. I ended up going to one of the more "relaxed" prestigious universities and decided to take it easy, which was good because otherwise it might have been soul-destroying.



What Prof. Jiang describes at 55:30 is 100% spot on. It would have helped me and the vast majority of my classmates to see it presented like this. During my time in high school, multiple of my peers either transferred out of the school for "medical reasons" (i.e. self-harm) or actually killed themselves. In the latter case, there was a big performative candlelight vigil, followed by the incident being immediately memory-holed -- dissociative af.


I hope that students and parents of students in these highly competitive environments can take a step back and consider what's really important (health, happiness, self-actualization) and what's not (the elites' fake and arbitrary depiction of "success").

You’re a Yale graduate, and currently teach at Tsinghua University High School, widely known as one of the top elite grooming institutions in China.
Historically, Tsinghua’s roots go back to Tsinghua College, which was funded using money from the Boxer Indemnity, war reparations China was forced to pay after the Boxer Rebellion.

The U.S. redirected those funds not back to China’s people, but toward establishing a prep school to send select Chinese students to Ivy League universities, especially Yale.
This created a pipeline where China's future elite would be educated abroad, not just academically, but ideologically.

This is part of a larger pattern:

Mao Zedong himself worked in the Yali-affiliated library, part of the Yale-in-China mission.

He was mentored by Yang Changji, who collaborated with Yale missionaries.

That environment seeded Mao’s exposure to Western revolutionary theory Marx, Bakunin, Kropotkin. Not organically, but strategically embedded.


When I now see your channel gaining traction mixing high-level geopolitical analysis with increasingly esoteric and symbolic language, I can’t help but wonder:

 Are we witnessing a new kind of archetype being shaped? A post-digital, spiritually-coded form of elite influence?
Is this a genuine awakening, or a groomed awakening one that feels liberating, but remains structurally contained?



There’s a concept called Managed Mass Awakening where difficult truths are revealed, but the direction, scope, and synthesis remain under invisible control.

I say this not to criticize, but to open reflection for you, and for your audience.
The deeper the truth, the more important it becomes to ask:

Who benefits from its timing?

What invisible frameworks remain untouched?

And are we thinking freely or inside a well-designed narrative chamber?

38:40 I just want to make a correction that student debt cannot be passed on to children. There is no form of debt in the USA which can be directly passed directly to your children. The debt can be taken out of the value of your eligible assets at the time of your death. Meaning if I owe $50,000 in student debt, but I had $50,000 in my savings account, my children will not get my inheritance until my debts have been paid. There are many mechanisms in the United States to subvert this process. You can set up trusts and keep valuable assets in the trust instead of in your personal name so that the inheritance of said assets can be immune from any of your debt. Most poor and middle class people do not utilize trusts though.

### **@ExenTrax** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JU_8fJjtGxA&lc=UgwuK_KSHP3q6NtOVOx4AaABAg)

While an interesting lecture, professor Jiang is misusing the term “disassociative personality disorder” (which isn’t actually a recognized clinical term at all).

He seems to be conflating Dissociative Identity Disorder (DID, formerly called “multiple personality disorder”) and the broader category of personality disorders. DID is specifically about fragmented identity states/alters, and doesn’t really map onto what he’s describing in the first place).

The traits the prof. is describing map much more cleanly onto a couple of real personality disorder diagnoses: 
	1.	Narcissistic Personality Disorder (NPD) maps the most strongly (especially the vulnerable narcissist subtype). The core features line up almost exactly: a deeply fragile self-esteem masked by ambition and superiority, a sense of entitlement that justifies rule-breaking, chronic emptiness, and self-worth entirely contingent on external achievement [the key insight in NPD is that the grandiosity is the defense against the insecurity, not a separate thing]
	2.	Borderline Personality Disorder (BPD). This maps onto the “void” piece specifically: chronic feelings of emptiness and near-suicidal reactivity to perceived failure. However, BPD is essentially defined by an unstable or absent stable sense of self and I do not think this is what prof. is gesturing towards as it is quite unlikely any university would actively seek out individuals with BPD. 

In practice, the profile he’s describing [achievement-as-identity, emptiness, amorality, fragile self-worth, etc.] would most likely be diagnosed as NPD, if severity reached a pathological extreme. However, universities likely aren’t seeking out narcissists at the extreme pathological severity level because this type can and often do cause serious problems with staff/faculty, other students, criminal activity, etc. both on-campus and throughout their lives after graduation. They carry significant risk related to reputational harm for institutions. 

All that being said, universities very well may seek out individuals who exhibit what’s informally called contingent/performance-based self-esteem taken to a relative extreme. They may also seek out individuals who exhibit some level of amorality /benevolent psychopathy / manipulative tendencies / ability to exploit systems in their favor, etc. [but, again, not to an extreme pathological level that would necesitate a personality disorder diagnosis before the age of 18 (or even come close to doing so). The risks of such students far outweigh the benefits in the vast majority of cases.

### **@tadtastic**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JU_8fJjtGxA&lc=UgwROLK3yHJ-U1xGPJB4AaABAg)

this lecture really might have just changed my life. ever since i transferred to university from community college, i've felt much more isolated (i have literally no friends) and just driven to succeed and do well, which i noticed as the subtext behind basically everything here (course materials, how students talk, how advisors talk, advice given, etc.)—i've felt i stopped learning for learning's sake. very "utilitarian" or practical mindset, as laid at near the end. i'm sure this is fueled by my trauma of my dad screaming at me for having bad grades on my progress reports and schools, and having my parents tell me i'll be successful, etc., not to mention the capitalist delusion of pulling yourself up by your bootstraps pervading society.

at the same time, i was thinking that i do all this so that i can help the world and be "altruistic." but it's really eye-opening to think these are mutually exclusive. all i know is being in a competitive university (not ivy league but still the success mindset is there) thus far has not made me happy. hearing all this is freeing in a way—it makes me feel like i can actually spend time just doing what i want to do rather than chase endless achievement. even though i don't really care for achievement, it's really like basically all i know given my experienced and environment.

i really gotta reflect on my life now. thank you, professor jiang.

### **@jere8187**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JU_8fJjtGxA&lc=Ugy5KypHGQvpTTxXtVd4AaABAg)

Great history here of the origins of the US system, along with a very provocative in-depth explanation of meritocracy. It's been easy to see for awhile that the US system is a doomed, ridiculous and evil system while this adds some new angles for me. 100 years ago I worked on Wall Street for a firm and I was the only non-Ivy leaguer in my fairly sizable department. I wondered why they hired me as I assumed Ivy Leaguers were the creme de la creme (likely it's that they poached me from their main competitor and I was willing to do work others wouldn't) and though I may have had potential but ultimately I was an average student at an average school. It was eye opening to be surrounded by and working with these Harvard, Yale and Princeton grads and realize they were nothing special. On a lark I applied to Harvard Graduate School and never heard back. I wouldn't have went even if I was accepted. Not even an acknowledgement though of my application. So I applied again. Still nothing. I called them and got blown off. Now I know why. I have a soul and I was traumatized as a youth but not nearly enough. 🙂

37:14 - "America is one of the most unequal societies in the world." Unequal in what? Height? Weight? Financial Security? Physical Beauty? Unequal in WHAT? Also - student debt hasn't increased so much due to "it's gotten more expensive" as for - the U.S. government stopped funding college education as they used to do. I got through college on Pell Grants, student aid programs, work/study programs, and about 5% of it was a 3% "student loan" through Wachovia. I was eligible for welfare and food stamps so I only had to work during the Summer. This was all bundled into a "Student Loan Package". At graduation, I was responsible for only about 8% of the total 4 year tuition (plus books that were cheap back then).  I paid it off in 4 months post-graduation. It was more a change in WHO PAID THE BILL than - oh it just got more expensive.

### **@LOL-it1pu**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JU_8fJjtGxA&lc=UgwHQSk0Q5Pbjp20eGN4AaABAg)

That there is no difference between Catholicism and Protestantism is simply untrue. The speaker’s claim that the only difference between the Catholic and Protestant churches is who leads them (Pope vs. King) is a serious distortion of history and theology. The Protestant Reformation was driven by a rejection of unbiblical doctrines such as papal authority, the veneration of Mary and the saints, purgatory, and transubstantiation, none of which have any foundation in Scripture. Instead, the Reformers upheld salvation by grace through faith alone, the authority of Scripture alone, and direct access to God without priestly or institutional mediation. Many paid for these truths with their lives: William Tyndale, for example, was executed for translating the Bible into English so ordinary people could read God’s Word for themselves. These were not superficial disagreements about church leadership, but deep, gospel-centered convictions worth dying for.

> **@LOL-it1pu**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JU_8fJjtGxA&lc=UgwHQSk0Q5Pbjp20eGN4AaABAg.AOLB5TFCNOJAOLC-bw42Cn)  
>
> I watched a bit more and have to add: the lecture inaccurately presents Protestant beliefs and early American religious migration. Protestants did not believe they could “act as God,” but that individuals could approach God directly through Scripture, without mediation by priests or kings. He also misidentifies “Dissenters,” which included a wide range of Protestant groups, not just Puritans, but also Quakers, Baptists, Separatists, and others, who left England for various theological and political reasons. While it’s true that education was central to many of these groups, and Harvard was founded to train ministers, the Ivy League as a collective concept didn’t exist at that time, and its name came much later in the 20th century. You can't oversimply things fo rthe sake of brevity.

### **@upridem**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JU_8fJjtGxA&lc=UgxkIWi8h9iSD49v3454AaABAg)

Professor Jiang, sua palestra me tocou fundo porque dá pra sentir o peso que você carrega. Você abre o jogo sobre o desespero que sentiu pra entrar em Yale, a pressão dos pais imigrantes, a sensação de ter que provar valor o tempo todo. Dá pra ver que isso ainda dói, e que essa dor virou uma lente que colore tudo: Yale vira arena cruel, o Ocidente vira máquina fria, e o mérito chinês vira o único caminho “puro”. Você fala com uma emoção tão crua que é impossível não se identificar com o sofrimento.

Mas, se me permite ser direto, acho que essa lente está te prendendo mais do que te libertando. Quando você conta sua história como prova de que o sistema é só simulacro e ambição disfarçada, acaba transformando sua própria superação em algo vazio. Você foi aceito em Yale — não só por “fome de riqueza” da instituição, mas porque alguém viu algo genuíno em você. Em vez de reconhecer isso como um ponto de luz, você o apaga pra reforçar a narrativa de que nada é real. E isso, sinceramente, parece estar te consumindo aos poucos.

O mais preocupante é o que isso faz com quem te escuta. Muitos jovens que te acompanham saem da palestra mais desesperados do que entraram: se até o professor que “venceu” o jogo diz que o jogo é falso e cruel, pra onde eles vão? Você acaba virando um espelho do abismo que critica, e isso multiplica o desespero em vez de oferecer saída.

Talvez o caminho seja mais simples do que parece: pare de tratar o desespero como a “verdade final” da sua vida. Ele foi parte da jornada, mas não precisa ser o fim dela. Tente olhar pra trás com um pouco mais de gentileza consigo mesmo: perdoe o garoto que se desesperou tanto pra ser aceito. Perdoe o sistema por ser falho e humano. E comece a contar sua história de um jeito diferente: “Eu entrei no simulador, fui engolido por ele, mas saí vivo — e descobri que humildade, compaixão e integridade valem mais que qualquer diploma ou nota.”

Você tem uma voz forte, uma análise afiada e uma coragem enorme pra se expor assim. Use isso pra mostrar que é possível atravessar o fogo e sair do outro lado inteiro, não só pra alertar que o fogo queima. Sua história ainda não terminou. Ela pode ser de redenção, não só de alerta. Tem gente torcendo por você... inclusive eu.

### **@LeoWang28** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JU_8fJjtGxA&lc=UgzafzMQoVcxvuoFKa94AaABAg)

Here, I fixed it for y'all - the professor’s use of “Dissociative Identity Disorder (DID)” is not accurate in a clinical sense.  
He is using the term metaphorically, not medically, and he goes far beyond what DID actually means.

Let me break down why.

🧩 1. What DID actually is (based on medical sources)
Dissociative Identity Disorder is a mental health condition involving:

Two or more distinct identity states

Memory gaps

Dissociation

A history of severe trauma

It is not:

Ambition

Insecurity

Desperation

Risk‑taking

“Breaking rules”

High achievement

None of those traits are diagnostic criteria.

So medically speaking, the professor’s usage does not match the definition.

🎓 2. What the professor meant when he said “DID”
In the lecture, he uses “DID” as a metaphor for:

Trauma

Insecurity

Hyper‑achievement

A drive to prove oneself

A willingness to take risks or break norms

He is not describing DID as clinicians understand it.
He is using the term loosely to describe a psychological profile of extreme drive.

This is not how the term is used in psychology or psychiatry.

⚠ 3. Why this is a problem
Using a clinical diagnosis as a metaphor for personality traits can be:

Inaccurate

Misleading

Stigmatizing

Confusing for students

It also blurs the line between:

Trauma‑driven ambition (a real phenomenon)

A dissociative disorder (a specific medical condition)

These are not the same thing.

🧠 4. What he could have said instead
If he wanted to describe the traits he was talking about, more accurate terms would be:

Trauma‑driven achievement

Compensatory ambition

Insecurity‑based striving

High‑risk/high‑reward personality traits

Overcompensation patterns

These concepts exist in psychology and do not misuse a clinical diagnosis.

🎯 5. So did he go too far?
From a clinical perspective: yes.  
He stretched the term far beyond its meaning.

From a rhetorical perspective:
He was using it to dramatize a point about:

elite institutions

trauma

ambition

selection pressures

But the term “DID” does not help his argument — it actually confuses it, because it introduces a medical label that doesn’t fit the traits he’s describing.

🧠 6. A more accurate interpretation of his argument
If we strip away the misused terminology, his core point seems to be:

Elite institutions select for people who are driven by insecurity, trauma, or a need to prove themselves — because those people often push themselves extremely hard and may achieve extraordinary things.

That idea can be debated, but it has nothing to do with Dissociative Identity Disorder.

She got into Duke, but somehow was waitlisted at UNC Chapel Hill. She ended up at Vanderbilt for ungrad and Emory for grad school. The system makes no sense: (Harvard hates kids from the South. We are supposed to stay in our lane, South of the Mason Dixon line). She's a corporate attorney in D.C. now. She's the most insecure person I know. She works for ppl much dumber than her, and she knows it. She doesn't have the right last name to move up the ladder any further. We are from a small southern town. My Dad is a smalltown defense attorney and a judge. Everyone knows him; He's a big fish in a very small pond. My sister is the opposite. She decided to swim with the sharks in the ocean that is the nation's capital, Washington D.C. and she's absolutely miserable but to move home would be to admit failure. She's 40 years old with no husband or kids. She's accepted that she will never have a family or meet a decent man in the circles she runs in, in D.C. If she moved home, she'd be a huge fish in a small pond, make just as much money, and her quality of life would improve drastically; yet, she refuses to even entertain that idea. You see..."They" tricked her...she believes she's part of it. She thinks she's one of "them" (the 1%). But she's not, and she's going to learn the hard way. She'll never become a partner at her lawfirm. She'll never be allowed to go any further in her career than she's at currently.. and when the sht hits the fan and the elite retreat to their bunkers, silos, and underground cities, there will be no seat available for her on the plane getting out of DC. She's extremely vain, arrogant, and fragile all at the same time. She is not very creative anymore, although she used to be when we were younger. Meeting social norms and expectations is the most important thing in the world to her.

Im the blacksheep. I went to a very good private high school (something my older sister never had an opportunity to do), then, I went to a very average state university where I played soccer on a full scholarship. School was never that important to me. I graduated high school with a 3.0 GPA. I barely cracked a book during high school. I got a 32 on my ACT. Never took the SAT, because I wasn't planning to apply to any schools that required it. All I wanted to do was play soccer. Somehow, I stumbled my way into becoming an engineer. I invested in crypto at the right time. Got very lucky and was able to purchase a home and other assets much quicker than my sister. She couldn't understand why I was seemingly doing better than her financially. We didn't speak for about 5 years until she bought a $1m condo near Georgetown. We both have DPD from a very tough childhood. Money was never an issue for us, but seemingly everything else was. My mother grew up very poor and she never changed her mindset. Message to everyone watching this and taking everything Professor Jiang says at face value -- this entire talk falls apart as soon as you do a small amount of research pertaining to the consistently innovative talent that Harvard produces. 

His main example of the failure of meritocracy here is that Harvard doesn't want smart Chinese students who are great at math. The truth is that the US recognised that academic achievement isn't the only MERIT from which we should judge. The US drives innovation across arts, culture, & industry. That innovation often comes from students who had mediocre academic performance. 

Meanwhile, in China, the students who's parents have high ranking positions in the CCP crap all over great students who don't, China is KNOWN for stealing international IP because they are not innovative on their own, and the Chinese minds who do become innovative & successful (Jack Ma, Sun Dawu, Bao Fan, to name a few) end up disappeared or imprisoned by the CCP with their assets stolen.
